because it just happened to be only note which was never actually
dropped. There seems to be quite some truth in this observation, because
in many jāti-born forms which shared a common musical scheme with the
jātis, madhyama could be dropped.297

Raghavan298 draws our attention to Utpala’s view which is cited under NŚ
29.28. The 29th adhyāya deals with the instrumental music relating to
the stringed instrument. According to Bharata, the musical figures
consisting of svaras, in theatric songs (dhruvās), generate excessive
charm when they are ascending and not confined to themselves.299 The
ascending svaras (ārohiṇaḥ svarāḥ) are those whose ascendance is
directly visible. Their excessive charm is due to the fact that each
syllable (akṣara) accompanies each svara and each svara accompanies each
syllable (akṣara).300 This was the view which was upheld by Utpala
against those who believed that the musical figures consisting of svaras
ought to be restricted to only a particular part.

Immediately hereafter Utpala’s authority is again invoked under NŚ
29.30. It is referred to by Pandey301 and Raghavan302 both. According to
Pandey the context relates to the ornaments (alaṁkāra) of song. Pandey
is right, since according to the context the musical figures consisting
of svaras here pertain to the second variety involving songs. Utpala is
cited in order to eliminate any doubt that even within the given context
of song-presentation (gītāprayoga) the musical figures that are solely
based on varṇas, by virtue of being constituents of the words (pada) (of
a melody), relate to svarālāpa alone and not to the rendering of the
song as such. The semblance of musical figure in a song is only a
derivative product of the residual impression of varṇas and hence
considered secondary.303

Relying upon the phrase vyācakṣate in the next citation Pandey suspects
that probably Utpala was a commentator of the NŚ or “on some parts of
it” (Pandey 1959: 540) which was debated by Raghavan (1981: 122). He
surmised that Utpala interpreted Bharata’s text, i.e. the last line of
the NŚ 31.26 (trayo bhedā hi tālasya dviguṇād [dviguṇā Raghavan]
dviguṇāḥ smr̥tāḥ), in the former’s independent work on music, an issue
which we have already visited at the outset of this paper. I am however
in favour of treading the middle path. Whether or not Utpala wrote an
independent work on music, Abhinavagupta’s copious allusions to him from
the opening chapter (i.e. 28th chapter) of the Geyādhikāra through 32nd
chapter as an authority whom he broadly follows, makes it a sensible
proposition to believe that Utpala might have commented upon the
Nāṭyasāstra’s section on music or at least a sizeable portion of it. Let
us leave the issue at that. Raghavan highlights one more significance of
this citation, i.e. to show that Abhinavagupta was not a blind follower
of his grand-teacher: on occasions he also used to differ from him.

Utpala’s statement and Abhinavagupta’s disagreement or corrective review
comes in the ABh on NŚ 31.26,304 which discusses the basic
tāta-structures in gāndharva music. Yathākṣara (lit. “corresponding to
the number of syllables”: sarvathākṣarasaṁkhyayā tulyā — ABh IV: 162) is
the technical name for the basic tāla-units. Caccatputaḥ305 and
cācapuṭaḥ are the two basic tāla-prototypes or yathākṣaras. Bharata
calls them yonidvaya (NŚ 31.7), because they were the base on which
other tāla or kalā modes were constructed.306 The terms caccatpuṭaḥ and
cācapuṭaḥ, meaningless by themselves, constituted word formulas (pada)
which, when analysed letterwise (akṣara) and metrically on the basis of
mātrās contained in each syllable, yielded the basic prototype of the
yugma and ayugma modes of grouping kalās. The yathākṣara syllabic
arrangement in yugma (caccatpuṭaḥ) was — guru, guru, laghu, laghu, pluta
and the pattern of tāla in terms of kalās was: one kalā + one kalā +
half a kalā + one and a half kalā. This pattern was to be applied in the
ekakala mode. Bharata gives three alternative beat arrangements (NŚ
31.11-15) for the caccatpuṭaḥ (yugma mode). The first alternative he
calls the śuddha alternative (NŚ 31.117), the only beat formation that
was further expanded with the addition of other beats to form dvikala
and catuṣkala modes. Kalā signified a unit of time. The five nimeṣa
mātrā was the standard of measuring kalā as a time unit in gāndharva. In
order to render an ekakala yathākṣara tāla into a dvikala, number of
metric yathākṣara mātrās were doubled and divided uniformly in terms of
gurus instead of the divisions into pluta, laghu, etc.: ‘ekā prakr̥tir
iti’ । gururūpo yo bhedaḥ so ’traikasvabhāve
laghuplutādicchedavibhāgaśūnyaḥ so ’tra prakr̥tir mūlabhūtaḥ (ABh on NŚ
31.7). Each guru as before was equal to one kalā; but in dvikalā two
such kalās made a single unit accounting for the mode’s naming as
dvikala. One unit of two kalās in the dvikala mode was termed the
padabhāga, and four such padabhāgas made a larger unit of tāla called
mātrā, which term in this sense had a strict technical connotation
confined to tāla-structures in gāndharva. The dvikala padabhāga had two
kalās. The catuṣkala was double the dvikala: dvirbhāvād dvikalasyāpi
vijñeyo ’tha catuṣkalaḥ । (NŚ 31.40). It had four kalās. Consequently,
the total number of kalās in a catuṣkala caccatpuṭaḥ was sixteen (4
padabhāgas of 4 kalās each).307

According to Abhinavagupta, the precise intention of Bharata is to show
that doubling (dvaiguṇya) of metric yathākṣara mātrās was the sole
factor for distinguishing the three mārgas, and not the tempo etc. as
such. Bharata speaks of three layas (tempos), viz. druta (fast), madhya
(medium) and vilambita (slow). These corresponded to the three mārgas:
citra mārga (druta laya), vr̥tta or vārttika mārga was in madhya laya
(medium tempo) and the dakṣiṇa mārga related to vilambita laya (slow
tempo). In the three mārgas there were three different styles of
applying the kalās. Thus in the citra mārga, the mode of
kalā-application was ekakala, in the vr̥tta mārga it was dvikala and in
the dakṣiṇa mārga it was catuṣkala.308 But what separated the three
mārgas was the doubling and on this ground even ekakala, that is,
principal yathākṣara mode, would also attract doubling. But a section of
musicologists differed. In their opinion, the first type, i.e. ekakala
or citra mārga alone, could boast of doubling proper, whereas the
remaining two, viz. dvikala and catuṣkala, for that matter vr̥tta and
dakṣiṇa mārgas, could be said to have doubling in successive order
(kramadviguṇau). It is only in this sense that all the tāla structures
could be said to be double (dviguṇa). It is at this point that Utpala’s
view is alluded to. Utpala had different explanation. According to him
though the precise reason for holding tālas to be of three kinds (viz.
ekakala, dvikala and catuṣkala) was due to mutually partaking of their
threefold character in between themselves, their threefold modal
outgrowth depended on the process or phenomenon called doubling. In
fact, the word yathākṣara owes its usage to the letterwise metrical
correspondence of the basic tāla pattern, hence by single doubling of
the metric yathākṣara mātrās (i.e. in ekakala mode) it could be expanded
into dvikala and by two doublings into catuṣkala (because the two kalās
in dvikala mode formed a single unit and two dvikalas formed a
catuṣkala). By virtue of being catuṣkala, and their being no more
divisions, there was no further multiplication. This view of Utpala
evoked a somewhat discordant note from Abhinavagupta who seems to extend
the scope of doubling to vīṇā (Indian lute) as well by ruling out
impossibility of doubling in the vīṇā.309

The next quote to which attention is drawn by Pandey310 and Raghavan311
occurs in the ABh on NŚ 32.4-6. The 32nd chapter deals with theatric
songs, i.e. dhruvā.312 Pandey notes the dhruvā connection of this quote.
Just in the immediately preceding verses, Bharata has laid down that the
components of the saptarūpa or gītakas, suitably transformed, resulted
in the dhruvās.313 Once transformed into dhruvās these gītaka components
acquired independent forms with their own character. Yet as explained by
Abhinava, there remained a cause and effect relationship between the old
and the new formations. Hence the components of the gītakas which had a
particular arrangement ordained for the achievement of adr̥ṣṭa, could be
reorganized with a new end in view: they could be suitably regrouped to
express rasas and moods. They were to this end, reorganized according to
the metrical structure of the song being sung. In this new regrouping, a
causal relation with the original gītaka composition, though present,
was not immediately perceived. The new group, moreover, could become the
base for futher formations.314

In the kārikās under current reference, i.e. NŚ 32.4-6, Bharata hands
out a list of such gītaka components converted into dhruvās such as
mukha saying that these formed the limbs of all the five types of
dhruvās and giving details of their specific functional use at the
different dramatic moments on the stage.315 The phala or end result of
an object (i.e. the purpose it serves, the fruit it bears) is connected
with its function which Abhinavagupta calls its dharma.316 The function
of gītakas, turned and transformed into dhruvās, was closely connected
with nāṭya. As to the doubt, what would be the end result of these
gītaka components — seen (dr̥ṣṭa), that is, identical with that of
dhruvās, or unseen (adr̥ṣṭa), that is which is present as a cause but is
not actually felt, i.e. one identical with the former gītakas.
Abhinavagupta thinks both the options — simultaneous pursuit of the
collective fruits or going in for either fruit alternatively — would be
futile because of attendant anomalies. Hence he resorts to the final
dictum of his grand-teacher Utpaladevapāda, who squarely held that their
precise purpose/function/end result was to evince their absolute
dependence upon or inspiration from their causal former status,
characterized by enduring continuity due to which they are transformed
into dhruvās and can be so discerned by the wise (prajñaḥ prakalpayet)
or by sharp intellect (prajñopakalpayet). The reason — dhruvā being an
effect, a product (kārya) — the original characterstic (dharma) inheres
in it like its cause.317

Incidently the last quote I could lay my hands on is found in the ABh on
NŚ 32.414.318 The kārikā and Abhinavagupta’s commentary are absolutely
technical and I have not been able to understand them.319 The 32nd
chapter deals with dhruvās and the kārikā under reference is from that
section of the chapter which particularly handles the method of
performing dhruvās (dhruvāprayoga). Bharata intends to initiate
discussion about how and when avanaddha instruments are to be used in
course of performing dhruvās320 in the pūrvaraṅga. Prāveśikī, as is
evident from the name, was used to indicate the entry of the characters
on the stage. According to Bharata the three encirclings (circular
movements, parivarta) were to be performed without the accompaniment of
the avanaddha instrument. It was only in the fourth encirclement
(parivarta) that the use of the avanaddha instrument was to be resorted
to. Abhinavagupta notes that it was Bharata’s view, because there were
others who enjoyed bhāṇḍa-less performance only in the initial circular
round, the next three were to be accompanied with bhāṇḍa (i.e. avanaddha
instrument).321 The question that with bhāṇḍa in use what was the
specific beat to begin the dhruvā singing was answered by the kārikā we
are directly concerned with. There could be one of three approaches
described as sannipātagraha, tarjanīgraha or ākāśagraha. The first
sounded beat was formed with both hands synchronously, the second by the
rhythmic strikings of the index finger and the third was a kind of empty
(comparable to khālī in modern Indian singing) beating, i.e. sounded
beating devoid of any specific lakṣaṇa. Abhinavagupta refers to the
existence of two contesting opinions in this regard. Some stated that
sannipātagraha was the proper beating for ekakalatāla, ākāśagraha for
dvikalatāla and tarjanīgraha for catuṣkalatāla. However, Abhinavagupta
firmly abided with the interpretation of Utpaladeva, his grand-teacher,
who held that bhāṇḍagraha precisely applied to singing (as if)
descending from ether, i.e. nothing, in the absence of precise guiding
rule as regards the specific tāla mode such as ekakala.322

Knowing fully well that these pages by no means exhaust Utpala’s
aesthetic insights in totality, I have to close it for now. I firmly
believe that there is enough in Utpala that needs to be explored
further. For instance Pandey makes an extremely important observation323
with regard to Abhinavagupta’s theory of aesthetics that in aesthetic
experience it is not the emotive aspect but the mental that has
preponderance and explains it in the light of Abhinavagupta’s treatment
in the ABh. However, I find that the seeds of this thesis could be
traced to Utpala. Similarly, it will be interesting to see as to what
Utpala has to say on beauty as an object, more so because aesthetic
experience is essentially beauty experience. I very much wanted to work
and enlarge on these issues but they need to form the subject matter of
some future paper.

* When professors Torella and Bäumer graciously asked me to contribute a
paper on Utpaladeva’s contribution and suggested that I revisit my paper
on error (“Theory of Error According to Abhinavagupta”, JIPh, 14, 1986),
I instead opted for exploring Utpala’s contribution to aesthetics, if
any. Little did I realize then that the area I chose demanded a lot more
than I could possibly give to it.

generation not only in the sphere of philosophy as did Lakṣmaṇagupta,
Abhinava’s teacher in philosophy, but also in the sphere of poetics, [.
. .] as did Bhaṭṭa Indurāja and Bhaṭṭa Tauta, Abhinava’s teachers in
Dhvani and Nāṭyaśāstra, respectively.” (Pandey 1935: 78.)

pratyabhijñāprasthānapravartayitr̥̥̄ṇām utpaladevapādānām । — Kaumudī on

11 Reproduced below is the relevant observation from Raghavan in full
(1981: 123):

“The Abhinavabhāratī is the only source of information as regards
Utpaladeva having written a work on music. Śārṅgadeva’s list and the
lists in other music works, cataloguing the saṅgītācāryas, do not
mention him. There seems to be however one reference, perhaps to this
work of Utpaladeva on music in the Śivatattva-Ratnākara of Basavarāja,
in his Saṅgīta Chapter. This work is a thesaurus like Someśvara’s
Abhilaṣitārtha-Cintāmaṇi otherwise called Mānasollāsa, to which the
Śivatattva-Ratnākara is much indebted. Chapter six of this work of
Basava treats of the fine arts. The concluding verses of this chapter
give the authorities of the Śāstras. The following refer to the Saṅgīta
Section —

dattilena kr̥taṁ nandibhr̥ṅgikohalakādibhiḥ ।

bharataṁ cādibharataṁ ca gītaratnākaraṁ tathā ।।

utpalādyaṁ parimalaṁ kr̥tiṁ śārṅgadharasya ca ।

Most likely the text of the last line is somewhat corrupt. Anyhow we
have a mention here, among authorities on Saṅgīta, of a name, Utpala.”
(In “Index” on p. 200, this work is shown as Utpalaparimala, but since
the Index [Authors, Works, Journals] was prepared posthumously, not much
value needs be attached to it.)

tadvaśād abhiniṣpannaṁ sarvaṁ vastu vibhajyate ।। — VP 1.117 (125)

[“It is this which is the basis of all the sciences, crafts and arts.
Whatever is created due to this can be analysed and communicated.” — tr.
Iyer, p. 112]. For Vr̥ṣabha’s analysis of Bhartr̥hari’s inherent art
process, see Rastogi 2009: 314-15 (specially n 69).

19 rūpaprasārarasato garhitatvam ayuktimat । — ŚD 1.12ab

20 In confining myself to the secular, I am guided by Abhinavagupta, who
fervently believes that the realm of aesthetics, though trans-mundane,
is not religious nor yogic. In the yogic there is absolute joy, bliss,
but no beauty experience, since beauty experience, in art or even in
nature, involves some intervening role of medium (see ABh, 1: 285). The
outcome of such an approach is visible in considering rasa-experience as
a kith and kin of the Absolutic experience (parabrahmāsvādasavidha) but
not the absolutic, religious or spiritual experience proper. For
detailed treatment see my “Abhinavagupta kī Saundarya-dr̥ṣṭi” in Rastogi
2012: 365-66.

40 kadā navarasārdrārdrasambhogāsvādanotsukam । — ŚSĀ 9.1. Kṣemarāja
glosses navarasārdrārdrasambhoga°: navarasena ārdrārdraḥ
nūtanabhaktiprasareṇa sātiśayaṁ spr̥haṇīyo samāveśātmā sambhogaḥ । —
ŚSĀ-Vr̥tti: 115; also see Rastogi 2012: 346.

41 itthamatyarthabhinnāvabhāsārthakhacite vibhau । samalo vimalo vā ’pi
vyavahāro ’nubhūyate ।। — ĪPK 1.7.14. My translation is influenced by
Pandey and Torella both. The word khacite is semantically very rich. In
Abhinavagupta’s exegesis it is uparakte (coloured). Both interpretations
— khacite and uparakte — carry pictorial and sculptural shades in their
meaning. Cf. ĪPV I: 390; ĪPVV II: 404: khacite svarūpānanyathābhāvena
uparakte. Torella also refers to it (1994: 146, n 28), but does not
discuss it.

42 māyāśaktyā bhedaviṣayo ’yaṁ sarvo vyavahāras tathājñānināṁ śuddho
’jñānāndhānāṁ tu malinas tattadabhinnārthāvabhāsabhāji bhagavati
saṁbhāvyate ’nubhavena । — Vr̥tti on ĪPK 1.4.14

43 na kevalam ete kāryakāraṇabhāvasmaraṇabādhāvyavahārāḥ
sakalalokayātrāsāmānyavyavahārabhūtā ekapramātr̥niṣṭhāḥ, yāvat
avāntaravyavahārā api ye krayavikrayādayaḥ samalāḥ,
upadeśyopadeśabhāvādayaś ca nirmalāḥ, te ’pi ekapramātr̥niṣṭhā eva
bhavanti । — ĪPV I: 389.

44 One could venture to describe it as nirmalo ’vāntaro vyavahāraḥ in
the light of Abhinavagupta’s treatment.

45 samalo māyāndhānāṁ krayavikrayavivādādiḥ nirmalaḥ
pratyabhijñātātmaparamārthānāṁ [. . .] viśeṣadr̥śi [. . .] śuddhātmanām
upadeśadīkṣānityanaimittikādyanuṣṭhāna- dhyānasamādhānādivyavahāraḥ [. .
.] vyavahārarūpatā sā hi bhedābhedaprāṇitasamanvayātmā sarvathā
bhedatirodhau tirodhīyate eva, tathāpi pratyabhijñātayāvatparamātmanāṁ
sa vyavahāraḥ svaparātmani malaśodhanabodhanaikadha(read gha)natvāt
vimala eva yuktaḥ । — ĪPVV II: 405

46 The basis for my conjecture is provided by the following phrases in
the ĪPVV: siddhāḥ, [. . .] niṣkalāḥ, [. . .] tadartham eva siddhaśabda
eṣāṁ viśeṣaṇam, tadā ca ādiśabdenaiva niṣkalātmano grahaṇam । — ibid.

47 “tathājñāninām” iti vr̥ttau samastaṁ padam । “saṁbhāvyate” iti vr̥ttāv
uktaṁ mūḍhānāṁ jhaṭiti tathābhimānābhāve ’pi uktopadeśapariśīlanadiśā
prakāśate eva ayam arthaḥ iti darśayitum । — ibid.

Utpala entertains the similar viewpoint in his ŚSĀ 12.24: vyavahārapade
’pi sarvathā pratibhātv arthakalāpa eṣa mām । bhavato ’vayavo yathā na
tu svata evādaraṇīyatāṁ gataḥ ।। [“Even in the realm of worldly
transaction let this mass of objects shine to me as thy limb in all
respects and not that it evokes regard for its own sake.”] [Also see n
281 below.]

48 tad uktaṁ śivavijñānopaniṣadi — “grāhyagrāhakasaṁvittiḥ sāmānyā
sarvadehinām । yogināṁ tu viśeṣo ’yaṁ saṁbandhe sāvadhānatā ।।” — VBh
106; iti । “kevalam” iti saty api sārvatrike māyāvyāpāre idamadhikam ity
arthaḥ । — ĪPVV I: 405.

49 anubhūyate ca sopadeśaiḥ avadhānaparaiḥ yena eṣāṁ yaiva saṁsāradaśā
saṁmatā vyavahāradaśā saiva pramātr̥tattvaprakhyātmikā śivabhūmiḥ ।
yaduktam ’saṁbandhe sāvadhānatā’ iti । — ĪPV I : 391

50 adhikāriṇo ’dhikā ’sti pratipattiḥ । — ABh I: 278. This idea of
adhika reminds me of Tagore’s notion of the “Surplus” that seeks its
outlet in the creation of art. Vide, Das 1996: 352. Except drawing
attention to the semiotic resemblance between the two ideas across the
vast historical spectrum there is neither my intention nor effort to
compare the two.

51 apravartitapūrvo ’tra kevalaṁ mūḍhatāvaśāt ।
śaktiprakāśen-eśādivyavahāraḥ pravartyate ।। [(“What in reality happens)
is only that the use in practical reality of notions and expressions
(°vyavahāraḥ) like ‘Lord’, which due to the condition of obfuscation was
not brought about earlier (apravartitapūrvaḥ) is promoted through
bringing to light of the powers”. — tr. Torella.] It may be noted that
īśādivyavahāra, in effect, amounts to nirmalatāvyavahāra.

52 kintu mohavaśād asmin dr̥ṣṭe ’py anupalakṣite । śaktyāviṣkaraṇeneyaṁ
pratyabhijñopadarśyate ।। [“However, since He, though being directly
perceived (dr̥ṣṭe ’pi) is not discerned for what He is because of
delusion, precisely for this reason, by bringing his Powers to light,
the recognition (pratyabhijñā) of Him is shown.” — tr. ibid.]

53 ĪPK 3.2.4.

54 yaḥ prasādalava īśvarasthito yā ca bhaktir iva mām upeyuṣī । tau
parasparasamanvitau kadā tādr̥śe vapuṣi rūḍhim eṣyataḥ ।। — ŚSĀ 8.1
[“When will that small amount of grace/ Abiding with the Lord / And that
small amount of devotion / That has come to me / Unite to become like
that unique form — / The blissful body of Śiva?” — tr. Bailly].

55 sarvathā nairmalyātmā prasādo bhagavadrūpatā । — ĪPVV I: 25-26

56 prasādo nirmalībhāvas tena saṁpūrṇarūpatā ।। ātmanā tena hi śivaḥ
svayaṁ pūrṇaḥ prakāśate । — TĀ 13.286-87

57 svecchāvabhāsitāśeṣalokayātrātmane namaḥ । śivāya śaśvad-
acchannasvacchasvacchandavr̥ttaye ।। — Benedictory verse of the Vivr̥ti,

prāptayaḥ parasparasaṁbandhāḥ । — ibid.

58 tena “śaśvad” iti kālaparicchedāsaṁbhavaḥ । “acchanna” iti
svarūpānatirekaḥ । “svaccha” iti anatiriktatve ’pi
atirikta-tābhāsasaṁbhavaḥ । “svacchanda” iti vimarśasvātantryayogaḥ ।
“vr̥tti”-padena eṣāṁ rūpāṇāṁ bhagavati śaktimati tādātmyāvabhāsa uktaḥ ।
[. . .] vr̥ttir vartanaṁ svarūpaspanditam eva yasya, sa eva [. . .] śivaḥ
parameśvaraḥ tasmai namaḥ iti [. . .] । — ĪPVV I: 7.

59 prabhur īśādisaṁkalpair nirmāya vyavahārayet ।।

Vr̥tti: svātantryāt [. . .] īśvaraḥ śivo veditā ity evamādivikalpair
ābhāsayati bhāvanādivyavahārārtham.

60 evam ekā kriyā saiṣā sakramāntarbahiḥsthitiḥ ।
ekasyai-vobhayākārasahiṣṇor upapāditā ।। — ĪPK 2.4.5. ĪPV adds (II:
160): sa caikaḥ āśrayaḥ saṁvidrūpatvena svacchatvasvatantratvābhyām
ubhayam apy antarbahīrūpaṁ sahate iti.

61 mohān manyamānasya moho ’pasāryate । — ĪPV II: 144. See for related
discussion, Rastogi 2012: 338-39.

62 tad āha “atiśayanirantara” iti । nairantaryam avadhānasya
vijātīyacittavyavahārābhāvo nairmalyam । — Vivr̥ti on ĪPVr̥ 1.4.2 (Torella
2007b: 548, text). It may not be difficult to revisit our initial
allusion to the notion of attentiveness in relationship in this regard.

63 śuṣkendhanāgnivat svacchajalavat sahasaiva yaḥ । vyāpnoty anyat
prasādo ’sau sarvatra vihitasthitiḥ ।। — KP VIII, cited in Kane 1951:
309.

64 kintu mohavaśād asmin dr̥ṣṭe ’py anupalakṣite । śaktyāviṣkaraṇeneyaṁ
pratyabhijñopadarśyate ।। [“However, since He, though being directly
perceived (dr̥ṣṭe’pi) is not marked (precisely noticed) because of
delusion by bringing (His) powers to light, this recognition (of Him) is
shown.” — tr. Torella, with modification]. This kārikā is sometimes
numbered as 1.1.2 taking ādiśloka (opening verse) separately from the
upodghāta (preface, foreword, introduction).

65 vītavighnatā, prakāśa and mohāpasāraṇa.

66 pratyabhijñāna, vimarśa, camatkāra and hr̥dayaṅgamatā.

67 dr̥ḍhaniścayarūpaṁ pratyabhijñānamātram । — ĪPVr̥ 1.2.3. Here “firm
ascertainment” (dr̥ḍhaniścaya) is not just an epistemic event, but
something more fulfilling — going direct to the heart. We discuss it
later.

68 ĪPK 4.1.17: aparijñātaḥ; 4.1.12: parijānataḥ and 4.1.3:
svasvarūpāparijñānamayo ’nekaḥ pumān mataḥ. Here Utpala construes
aparijñāna as finitization (parimitatvakaraṇa of the infinite,
pervasive) and reduces it to apratyabhijñāna: etad eva viśvātmanaḥ
parimitatvakaraṇam apratyabhijñānam ucyate । — Vr̥tti on 4.1.3. Thus in
Utpala’s view aparijñāna is the fragmented knowledge of the Universal
Self and pratyabhijñāna, by reverse implication, amounts to the
knowledge of the unfragmented, infinite or limitless reality. In fact,
defragmentation here qualifies knowledge or knowledge act.

69 ĪPK 3.1.7: tasyaiśvaryasvabhāvasya paśubhāve prakāśikā । vidyāśaktiḥ
[. . .] ।। Prakāśikā, vidyāśaktiḥ is glossed with svātmanaḥ
pratyabhijñānaṁ vidyayā in the Vr̥tti. It may be read in conjunction with
vidyābhijñāpitaiśvaryaḥ (ĪPK 3.2.2) glossed by the Vr̥tti as
ātmatattvābhijñayā. Abhinavagupta also connects abhijñā with
pratyabhijñā in ĪPV on 3.2.2 (II: 247): pratyabhijñāpadaṁ prāpitam
aiśvaryaṁ yasya saḥ.

70 This is the meaning assigned by Abhinavagupta. Though Utpala
frequently uses the word anusandhāna in the sense of unification,
co-ordination, mutual expectancy but expressly not in this sense.
However, Abhinavagupta assigns this meaning in an exclusive literary
context, explaining the word pratyabhijñeyam used by Ānandavardhana (DhĀ
1.8), on the explicit testimony of his master Utpala’s ĪPK 4.1.17.
According to him it is not a mere mechanical knowledge of the already
known but a kind of reconstructing, reinventing or reinterpreting,
capable of numberless possibilities providing the metaphysical basis for
the theory of dhvani “suggested meaning”. The format acquired by this
pratyabhijñāna is “this is such or of this kind” and not the usual kind
“this is the same thing”: pratyabhijñeyaśabdena idam āha [. . .] idam
ittham iti viśeṣato nirūpyamāṇaṁ sahasraśākhībhavati । yad uktam
asmatparamagurubhiḥ śrīmadutpalapādaiḥ “tais tair apy upayācitaiḥ [. .
.] tatpratyabhijñoditā ।।” (ĪPK 4/1/17) iti । tena jñātasyāpi viśeṣato
nirūpaṇam anusandhānātmakam atra pratyabhijñānam । na tu tad evedam idam
ity etāvanmātram । (DhĀL, pp. 35-36). Such a construction, if I may say
so, reminds us of the attributive recognition (dharma-pratyabhijñāna)
being the second variety in Śivopādhyāya’s twofold classification into
the substantive and the attributive recognitions. What must not be
missed is that Śivopādhyāya traces this division to ĪPK 4.1.12 (sarvo
mamāyaṁ vibhavaḥ, etc.) and its confirmation to ĪPV (II: 303), cf.
VBh-Vr̥tti, p. 96. By naming them as dharmi- and dharma-pratyabhijñāna
the attention of the scholars was drawn to this by me about three
decades back (Rastogi 1977-78: 856).

According to Abhinavagupta, Ānandavardhana prescribes this careful
inspection and constant investigation of word and meaning as mandatory
aspect of a great poet’s education. This is no doubt Ānanda’s primary
intention, as also confirmed by Abhinavagupta. But Jagannath Pathak
(2003: 96, n 2) shows that Ānanda’s language could be understood to
secondarily imply that it should be part of the qualified reader’s or
critic’s education as well.

This is becoming a very lengthy note, but for the benefit of the readers
I am reproducing below Ingalls’s translation of the DhĀ and Locana (pp.
124-25), of the relevant portion:

“K 1.8. [DhĀ (kārikā)]: This meaning and whatever particular word has
the capability of conveying it are the meaning and the word which should
be carefully scrutinized (or recognized, pratyabhijñeyau) by a great
poet.

A [Ānandavardhana (prose)]: The suggested meaning and the particular
word that has the capability of conveying it, not just any word: this
word and meaning should be scrutinized (or recognized) by a great poet.
It is by the proper use of the suggested sense and the word that
suggests it that a great poet deserves his name, not by mere structuring
of the denoting word and the denoted meaning.

L [Locana] should be scrutinized (or recognized): [. . .] And by the
suffix’s use in this mandatory sense he indicates that this [seeking out
of the suggestive word and sense] forms of [a poet’s] education. By
using the word pratyabhijñeyau he would indicate that although poetry
may flash forth (parisphurati) of its own accord in the way described
[by Bhāmaha 1.5] “poetry comes to the man of genius, and at that only
sometimes”; still, it increases in a thousand ways if that man will keep
considering his poem carefully, thinking, “this should be like this”
[i.e.: “I should say such and such, and not such and such”, in this way
always seeking the suggestive word and sense] (Brackets mine as this
constitutes editors’ explanation and not Abhinavagupta’s text). The
matter was put forth as follows by my teacher’s teacher, the renowned
Utpala: “As some lover brought by many prayers to a lady’s side, only to
find that she does not recognize him when he is come and so all hope of
making love to her [? read ‘him’] is gone; just so is God, although he
be our very soul, misprised within us and cannot share with us his
glory. Therefore I have written this book called Recognition.”

One sees from this that pratyabhijñā (recognition, scrutiny) is a
careful inspection of and continuous reflection upon an object although
that object is already [in some sense] known. This is what is meant by
pratyabhijñā and not the mere recognition that consists in noting that
“this is the same thing I saw before”.

Two more points merit attention here. One, no special effort is needed
to see that this kind of formulation of recognition seems to be implicit
in or inspired by the very theorization of recognition in “noticing” the
already “perceived” which hitherto remained “unnoticed” in the
immediately preceding section. Two, it also reconnects us with the
“doctrine of viśeṣa” alluded to during the course of configuring
aesthetic activities as forming human transaction of the pure category.
I have tried to show it elsewhere (Rastogi 2012: 151) that the idea of
pratyabhjñā, though not found directly in Kallaṭa, Vasugupta or
Vijñāna-Bhairava, was acquiring shape in particular direction. The idea
of a thing perceived indistinctly at first becoming distinct later on
keen re-observation due to exertion of one’s inner strength in SpK 3.4
(yathā hy artho ’sphuṭo dr̥ṣṭaḥ sāvadhāne ’pi cetasi । bhūyaḥ sphuṭataro
bhāti svabalodyogabhāvitaḥ ।।) and the acute mindfulness distinguishing
the yogic perception as regards subject–object relationship in VBh 106
(grāhyagrāhakasaṁvittiḥ sāmānyā sarvadehinām । yogināṁ tu viśeṣo ’sti
saṁbandhe sāvadhānatā ।।) seem to have had direct bearing on the
evolution of the notion of Pratyabhijñā into this particular mode.
Otherwise there was apparently no reason for Ānandavardhana, who was a
fellow courtier of Kallaṭa in the court of Avantivarman to have resorted
to this specific conceptualization of dhvani (i.e. yatnataḥ
pratyabhijñeyau tau śabdārthau mahākaveḥ ।। — DhĀ 1.8) and for
Abhinavagupta to have opted for this particular formulation of
Pratyabhijñā directly invoking the authority of Utpala from outside the
skirts of poetics in a solitary allusion to him throughout the Locana.
It seems that Abhinavagupta views this kind of abstraction as
constituting general parameter of real knowing. In the opening remarks
of the 16th chapter of the Gītārtha-Saṁgraha he describes such a
knowledge as vicāra, vimarśa or parāmarśa that is constituted by rise of
knowing marked by reasoning and reflection post-instruction: bodhaś ca
nāma śrutimayajñānānantaram ’idam ittham’
ityevaṁbhūtayukticintābhāvanāmayajñānodayena
vicāravimarśaparāmarśādirūpeṇa [. . .] bhavati । (BhGAS, p. 157) and
connects it with the reflection in totality coming later in the Gītā
(XVIII: 63: vimr̥śyamevamaśeṣeṇa) — ibid. Therefore in all fairness,
credit must be given to Abhinavagupta for demonstrating that from tad
evedam (“it is indeed that”) to idam itthaṁ (“it is like this”) to idam
etādr̥k (“it is such and such” — ĪPK 2.3.1). Utpala is intent upon
providing a unified level field from metaphysics to epistemology to
aesthetics transforming whole cognition into recognition and perception
of poetic meaning into the recognitive apperception of poetic meaning
through the discovery of poetic word. (See Rastogi 2012: 170 and also
Rastogi [2013a]: chapter on pramāṇa.)

71 The manner in which Utpala demonstrates the notion of Pratyabhijñā
highlighting its aesthetic nuance reminds us of the analogous
linguo-semantic treatment meted out to the word recognition in English.
Recognition comes from Latin verb cognoscere, to know, understand; thus
recognize is to know again, and incognito, unknown. Similarly the same
Latin verb cognoscere gives us connoisseur, gourmet, cognoscente,
aesthete (sahr̥daya). (Vide Reader’s Digest Family Word Finder, 1975, p.
174.)

72 kevalam asya svasaṁvedanasiddhasyāpi (glossing dr̥ṣṭe’pi) īśvarasya
māyāvyāmohād ahr̥dayaṅgamatvād [. . .]। Abhinavagupta remarks that this
causal ablative glosses the locative anupalakṣite which is therefore a
nimittasaptamī: nimittasaptamyarthas tu ahr̥dayaṅgamatvāt iti
hetupañcamyā vyākhyātaḥ । — ĪPVV I: 80 (see also Torella 1994: 3, text
note 22)

73 It may be noted in passing that Abhinavagupta in the ĪPV faithfully
echoes the Vr̥tti in treating anupalakṣite as hr̥dayaṅgamībhāvam aprāpte.
Abhinavagupta also sees a close connection between ĪPK 1.1.3 and 4.1.17
(the kārikā that Abhinavagupta cites to interpret Ānandavardhana’s
dhvani and uses here as an illustration) in holding that this appeal to
heart (or heart’s conviction) leads to the effective realization of the
internal causal efficiency which consists in pleasure or joy essentially
in the nature of relish equated with repose in the subject. The
actualization of this kind of causal efficiency depends upon emergence
of absolute conviction in one’s being ontologically full and complete.
Recognition remains the sole tool for effecting such a conviction:
anupalakṣite sarvathā hr̥dayaṅgamībhāvam aprāpte ata eva
pūrṇatāvabhāsanasādhyām arthakriyām akurvati,
tatpūrṇatāvabhāsanātmakābhimānaviśeṣasiddhaye pratyabhijñā [. . .] — ĪPV
I: 58; dvividhārthakriyāsti, bāhyā cāṅkurādikā,
pramātr̥viśrānticamatkārasārā ca prītyādirūpā [. . .]
pramātr̥viśrāntisārārthakriyā pratyabhijñānena vinā na dr̥ṣṭā [. . .]। —
ibid., II: 312-13. Also see ĪPV I: 220; ĪPVV II: 216.

I want to show that Abhinavagupta’s entire art speculation develops
around the central notion of heart. The total, dynamic and interactive
involvement of heart at every stage of art creation, experience and
appreciation, the starting point of his theory of art, is what
Abhinavagupta takes straight from Utpala. We must acknowledge that the
idea of arthakriyā envisaged right above is Abhinavagupta’s own
reduction and apparently would run counter to Utpala’s known stand that
internality being conceived in terms of identity with the subject would
have no space for causal efficiency which could logically be justified
in the realm of differentiated external reality only (āntaratvāt
pramātraikye naiṣāṁ bhedanibandhanā । arthakriyāpi bāhyatve sā
bhinnābhāsabhedataḥ ।। — ĪPK 1.8.6). In Abhinavan synthesis, however,
all cultural pursuits — viz. philosophical, spiritual, religious,
aesthetic and the like — comprise nirmala vyavahāra. And in virtue of
their constituting vyavahāra per se they, of necessity, must have some
arthakriyā, some immediate purpose to achieve. In so doing they graduate
from the domain of differentiated reality to the domain of
differentiated–undifferentiated reality tilted more towards the
undifferentiated, enlivened as they are by the underlining unity
furnished by this arthakriyā — it is arthakriyā because it is actuated
by conviction in or perception of one’s holistic and absolute identity,
which could now be theorized as internal — by reason of its being of the
nature of repose in the experiencing subject, standing for pleasurable
and joyous experiences. It is to be noted, by employing ādi (in
prītyādirūpā) Abhinavagupta renders pleasurable experience into a much
broader and more inclusive category so as to cover all experiences in
evenly situated varied contexts.

(ĪPK, ĪPVr̥, ĪPViv) duly coordinated by the ĪPVV:

upadarśyate ।

(a) (upadarśyate from kārikā is repeated in the Vr̥tti without

(b) According to ĪPVV I: 80, dr̥ṣṭe ’pi anupalakṣite is construed as

Abhinavagupta in order to lend further precision compares upadarśana
with “urging or prompting to be fully concentrated” (avadhāna-dāpana,

79 niviḍanijamohasaṁkaṭakāriṇā [. . .]
sattvodreka-prakāśānandamaya-nijasaṁvidviśrāntilakṣaṇena [. . .] bhogena
। — ABh I: 277

80 (ahr̥dayaṅgamatvād) asādhāraṇaprabhāvābhijñānakhyāpanena
(dr̥ḍhaniścayarūpapratyabhijñānamātraṁ) upadarśyate । — ĪPVr̥ on 1.1.3.
The word prabhāva is somewhat peculiar. Ordinarily it indicates impact,
influence. However, according to Abhinavagupta, it stands for
distinctive trait solely responsible for precise identification in the
first place and also for the exclusive function to be discharged by that
entity: prabhāvaḥ — prakarṣeṇa yadvaśāt bhāvyate, anyavilakṣaṇatayā ca
kartavyaṁ bhāvayati iti । — ĪPVV I: 86. Literally the word should denote
prabhutā (lordliness) or the highly abstracted, that is the essential,
state of being — prakr̥ṣṭa bhāva, i.e. svabhāva. In this case, powers of
the self or Lord — knowledge and action, jñāna and kriyā — transpire to
be his/His abstracted essence. The word asādhāraṇa provides exclusivity.
It appears that Utpala inherited this word (i.e. prabhāva) from his
teacher Somānanda: yathā nr̥paḥ sārvabhaumaḥ prabhāvāmodabhāvitaḥ । — ŚD
1.37cd. Utpala glosses prabhāva as aiśvarya in his Padasaṁgati:
aiśvaryacamatkāravāsitaḥ । — p. 29

81 My deduction is based on Abhinavagupta’s reformulation of “noticing”
(upalakṣaṇa) in terms of “bringing about camatkāra” (camatkārāpatti)
while explaining the word pūrṇa in the Vivrt̥i: (“pūrṇa” iti [. . .]
vimarśas tatsvabhāve yaś camatkāraḥ [. . .] tasya yā āpattiḥ [. . .] vā
yā camatkārāpattiḥ [. . .] tayā upalakṣitaḥ । — ĪPVV I: 85) and Utpala’s
other presentations. See p. 150 and n 142 also.

। — ibid.

Indirect: ĪPVV on ĪPK 1.1.3, 1.5.11, 13; 2.4.20; 2.3.7-8.

86 (a) yadā tu tasya ciddharmavibhavāmodajr̥mbhayā। — ŚD 1.7cd; ŚDVr̥ p.
10: āmodaś camatkāraḥ; (b) yadā nr̥paḥ sārvabhaumaḥ prabhāvāmodabhāvitaḥ
।। — ŚD 1.37cd; ŚDVr̥: 29: prabhāvāmodabhāvitaḥ [yathā]
aiśvarya-camatkāra-vāsitaḥ.

87 vibhavāmodabāhulyam asyāthavoktā nimittatā । — ŚDVr̥ on 3.95ab; ŚDVr̥:
139-40: [viśvātmatāprasaraṇarūpavibhavā]’’svādo [’parisamāpto . . .].

88 yāti vyāptiṁ tathābhūtāṁ śāmyad āmodasatkriyām । śāntānandavapuḥ
svacchaḥ urarīkr̥tadr̥kkriyaḥ ।। — ŚD 7.20. Utpala’s commentary on this
portion is lost, hence no view can be formed as to Utpala’s stand. As
regards Somānanda, it pertains to the stage following the state of
“lysis in the perfect consciousness” (cillaya) where the
blissful/refined activity in the form of āmoda gets somewhat subdued or
calms down.

89 Compare pratyavamarśaḥ svātmacamatkāralakṣaṇaḥ (ĪPV I: 250) which is
glossed by Bhāskara: svātmacamatkāralakṣaṇaḥ aham iti
svaviṣayāsvādarūpaḥ । — Bhās. I: 250. In Somānanda this state represents
the initial moment of will, i.e. consciousness (ŚD 1.8).

90 Soma’s bhāvitaḥ is glossed with vāsitaḥ (infused/informed with,
perfumed by, seasoned with, steeped in).

91 tathā prabhuḥ pramodātmā krīḍaty evaṁ tathā tathā ।। — ŚD 1.38 cd.

92 ŚD 1.12ab; also see above nn 86-88.

93 harṣānusārī spandaḥ krīḍā । — ŚDVr̥: 29

94 tasyāmodaś camatkāras tathāsvarūpaparāmarśarūpas tasya jr̥mbhā
viśvātmatayā vikasanam । yad uktaṁ mayā stotre “[. . .] viśvam āmr̥śasi
rūpamāmr̥śan । yatsvayaṁ nijarasena ghūrṇase [. . .]” (ŚSĀ 13.15) iti ।
ghūrṇanaṁ jr̥mbhoktā । — ŚDVr̥: 10. I confess my inability to
satisfactorily render ghūrṇana in English. There is a word in Hindi,
i.e. jhūmanā, which seems quite close to ghūrṇana. Jhūmanā represents a
rhythmic swinging movement of head and body indicating rapturous and
ecstatic state of mind. This particular formulation could have been
avoided but for the fact that Abhinavagupta makes a lot of this
formulation while discovering certain interconnectivities to which I
will have occasion to point out (p. 148), and also the reason that this
verse immediately follows the verse (i.e. ŚSĀ 13.14), where Utpala makes
a pointed reference to the notion of camatkāra. Moreover, Kṣemarāja in
his Vivr̥ti faithfully recaptures Utpala’s spirit and word in the ŚDVr̥
under reference: tathāsvarūpam āmr̥śan, nijaṁ svarūpaṁ camatkurvan
nikhilaṁ viśvam āmr̥śasi āsvādanena ānandaghanaṁ ghaṭayasi । yaś ca
svayaṁ nijena cidrasena ghūrṇase pūrṇatvāt samucchalattayā spandase । —
p. 206.

95 mahāmantramayaṁ naumi rūpaṁ te svacchaśītalam । apūrvāmodasubhagaṁ
parāmr̥tarasolvaṇam ।। — ŚSĀ 2.26. āmoda is glossed with parimala by
Kṣemarāja: apūrveṇa āmodena, alaukikena vyāpinā parimalena hlādinā
svarūpeṇa — ŚSĀ-Vivr̥tī, p. 34. Kṣemarāja’s immediate justification would
lie in complying with Utpala’s implied imagery of “clean, cool, fragrant
and pleasant” form of Śiva. However, camatkāra would be more natural
with mahāmantramaya and parāmr̥tarasolvaṇa rūpa of the Lord.

96 antarbhakticamatkāracarvaṇāmīlitekṣaṇaḥ । namo mahyaṁ śivāyeti
pūjayan syāṁ tr̥ṇāny api ।। — ŚSĀ 5.15 [“With my eyes closed / Repeatedly
savouring (lit. chewing) the relish of my inner devotion / May I worship
even the blades of grass thus: / Homage to Śiva, that is me.”] — tr.
Bailly with minor modifications.

97 yatsamastasubhagārthavastuṣu sparśamātravidhinā camatkr̥tim । tāṁ
samarpayati tena te vapuḥ pūjayanty acalabhaktiśālinaḥ ।। — ŚSĀ 13.14
[“That which bestows on all objects of beauty / The property of giving
marvellous taste at the mere touch — / By that very principle do those
endowed with / Unwavering devotion / Worship your form.” — tr. Bailly
with minor modifications].

As I have indicated earlier, I do not equate camatkr̥ti with wonder —
notwithstanding some amount of marvelling inherent in it — as regards
the Kashmir Śaivite scheme. Here, for the expression subhagārthavastuṣu,
Kotru’s translation “to all objects of fascinating significance” (p. 80)
is closer to Kṣemarāja and Rājānaka Lakṣmaṇa (pp. 204-05:
subhagaprayojanāny eva samastāni vastūni teṣu viṣayabhūteṣu — Kṣemarāja;
sundara prayojana vālī sabhī vastuoṅ ke viṣaya meṁ — Rājānaka Lakṣmaṇa),
but Bailly’s rendering “on all objects of beauty” seems to me nearer to
Utpala. In this case I will construe the text as subhageṣu arthavastuṣu
as compared to subhagārtheṣu vastuṣu, presumably adopted by Kṣemarāja
and Rājānaka Lakṣmaṇa.

98 prakāśasya mukhya ātmā pratyavamarśaḥ taṁ vinā artha-bheditākārasya
apy asya svacchatāmātraṁ na tv ajāḍyaṁ camatkr̥ter abhāvāt । [Reflective
awareness is the primary essence of light. In the absence of that,
though light might have form(s) differentiated by objects, it would have
mere lucidity but no sentience (lit. non-insentience), because of the
lack of savouring.]

99 (nanu vimarśābhāvāt jaḍatā syāt iti vaktavye) “camatkr̥ter abhāvāt”
iti (kathaṁ vr̥ttiḥ/camatkr̥tir hi bhuñjānasya yā kriyā bhogasamāpattimaya
ānandaḥ, sa ucyate ity āśaṅkyāha) “sa eva ca” iti (yaḥ prakāśasya
prāṇatvena uktaḥ) । [. . .] (tasmāt yuktam āha) “camatkr̥ter abhāvāt” ।
[. . .] “viśeṣaprītiḥ” (ity atra) “viśeṣapratītir” iti (apareṣāṁ pāṭhaḥ)
। “ata eva” iti (yato vimarśa eva camatkāraḥ, sa eva ca ajāḍyaṁ tato
hetoḥ) [. . .] pramātary eva viśrāmyati । — ĪPVV II: 177-79

100 Mark the discussion that begins with citation from the Vr̥tti:
(“camatkr̥ter abhāvāt” iti kathaṁ vr̥ttiḥ) ends in “tasmāt yuktam āha
camatkr̥ter abhāvāt.” Vide note above.

101 This formulation of camatkāra comes from Abhinvagupta, but I am
tempted to ascribe it to Utpala. The Vivr̥ti does not define camatkāra.
In fact, despite the frequent positing of the term camatkāra nowhere it
is defined directly by Utpala except once in the Vr̥tti (4.1.6) in terms
of activity replete with supreme light (of consciousness) and bliss or
the luminous blissful activity (paramaprakāśānandamayī kriyā) (see n 141
also). Abhinavagupta’s formulation comes as an intermediary remark
contextualizing the citations from the Vivr̥ti between camatkr̥ter abhāvāt
and sa eva ca. The force of sa eva ca (i.e. “and that very ānanda”, that
is the same as the activity of a savouring agent: bhuñjānasya kriyā),
whose instant referent is Abhinavagupta’s definitive explanation, lends
support to my conjecture, howsoever hypothetical it might appear to be.
Besides, Utpala’s single “once only” formulation alluded to above might
also be relied upon as lending direct support.

102 See n 99 above.

bhoktr̥tāmaye avicchinne bhuñjānatātmani camatkāre rūḍhiḥ ।

— ĪPVV II: 175

pramātr̥tāviśrāntir eva tata eva hr̥dayena parāmarśalakṣaṇena prādhānyāt
vyapadeśyā vyavasthitasyāpi prakāśabhāgasya vedyaviśrāntasya anādaraṇāt
sahr̥dayatā ucyate । — ibid.

107 nirvighnāḥ svādarūpāś ca rasanātad(emend rasanāḥ tad°)gocarīkāryāś
cittavr̥ttayo rasā nava [. . .] । — ibid.: 179. This seems to be the
portion of the text which Abhinavagupta identifies by uktam anyatra in
his ĪPVV on 2.4.20 (III: 251): °vighnavirahita eva āsvādo rasanātmā
camatkāra iti uktam anyatra. See n 116 below.

108 Rastogi: “Quintessentiality of Camatkāra in Rasa-experience:
Revisiting Abhinavagupta”, in Rastogi (ed.), 2013b: 438.

109 saṁvedanarūpatānāntarīyakatvena avasthitasya svatantrasyaiva
rasanaikaghanatayā parāmarśaḥ paramānando nirvr̥tiś camatkāra ucyate । —
ĪPVV I: 179. I venture to make a conjecture here that by equating
camatkāra with nirvr̥ti, Abhinavagupta is trying to restore Utpala’s
connection with Somānanda. Similarly, equation with paramānanda connects
him with the VBh and Bhaṭṭa Nārāyaṇa’s SC. See in the sequel, specially
n 145 below.

112 vāstave ’pi cidekatve na syād ābhāsabhinnayoḥ ।
cikīrṣālakṣaṇaika-tvaparāmarśaṁ vinā kriyā ।। [Even if the unity of
consciousness is maintained to be the only ultimate reality, there
cannot be action, for two entities divided as regards the nature of
their manifestation (ābhāsabhinnayoḥ), without a preliminary act of
thought which grasps and establishes the unity (ekatvaparāmarśaṁ)
characterized by the desire to act. — tr. Torella]

113 jaḍasyāpy asti bhavati ity asyām api sattākriyāyāṁ bubhūṣāyogena
svātantryābhāvāt akartr̥tvam । [An insentient reality cannot even be the
agent of the action of being — “(it) exists, is” — since it does not
possess the freedom that is manifested through “willing to be”
(bubhūṣāyogena). — tr. Torella, with modification]

114 (tat kathaṁ vr̥ttau uktaṁ) “svātantryābhāvāt” iti । (atra āha)
“svātantryam” ityādi [. . .] (tac ca jaḍānāṁ nāsti iti yuktaiva yuktiḥ)

“svātantryābhāvāt” iti । (prakāśasya ca paradaśāyāṁ camat-kāramātrātmā
yo vimarśas tad eva svātantryaṁ) [. . .]; parāparatve tu [. . .]
aparadaśāyāṁ tad eva [. . .] iti darśayati) “prakāśo’pi” (ityādinā)
“cikīrṣaiva” (ityantena) । “camatkāro hi” iti । [. . .] “camad”* iti [.
. .] [. . .] camatkāra iti uktam anyatra । — ĪPVV III: 251.

* The editors of the KSS edition do not print camad in bold and take it
as part of Abhinavagupta’s text. It could be so, but since camad is
immediately coming after camatkāro hi and is followed by iti, then
followed by Abhinavagupta’s extensive hermeneutics of the word, I
tentatively take it as a part of Utpala’s text with a question mark.

From here onwards the next leg of the Vivr̥ti begins. The relevant
portion is given below: “evam” iti [. . .] (paradaśāyāṁ nāsti
icchāvyavahāra iti [. . .] uktvā [. . .] śivadr̥ṣṭimatāvalambanena āha)
“athavā” iti । [. . .] “saiva hi iti ।” (camatkāritā hi [. . .] sarvatra
icchā । ) [. . .] “ādi”**grahaṇāt (abhilāṣamalaṁ) [. . .] (evam anyad
anusartavyam) “tadabhāvaś ca” iti (svātantryābhāvasya nimittam ukta
icchābhāva iti saṁgatiḥ ।) — ĪPVV III: 251-52.

** As per printed edition ādi is part of Abhinavagupta’s commentary. But
this has no meaning — it has to be a part either of the Vr̥tti or the
Vivr̥ti. Since it is not there in the Vr̥tti, its presence in the Vivr̥ti
should be presumed.

(ed.), 2013b: 436-37.

120 “evam” iti parimāṇaśūnye paradaśāyāṁ nāsti icchāvyavahāra iti

139 “parameṣṭhinaḥ” iti (vyākhyāya) “sāratayā” iti (sūtre yad anuvadanaṁ
hetutayā hr̥dayatve sādhye, tat vyācaṣṭe) “yathā” iti “cetanaṁ pratiṣṭhā”
iti (saṁbandhaḥ) “cetanāvatāmapi” iti “yathā” iti (saṁbandhaḥ) [. . .]
(prakr̥te yojayati) “tathaiva” iti । “viśvacetanasya” iti (bhagavataḥ
paramaśivasya ity arthaḥ । sā parā vimarśaśaktiḥ parameśvarasya)
“pratiṣṭhā” iti (anena śabdena parameśvarasya) “hr̥dayam” iti (anena ca
śabdena vācyā) । — ĪPVV II: 205.

It may be seen, Utpala is here revisiting his ĪPK 1.1.4 (tathāhi
jaḍabhūtānāṁ pratiṣṭhā jīvadāśrayā । jñānaṁ kriyā ca bhūtānāṁ jīvatāṁ
jīvanaṁ matam ।।). The reader will do well to remember that, according
to Abhinavagupta, 1.1.4 marks the technical starting point of the ĪPK
text (vide ĪPV I: 61 thereon: “tathāhi” iti yuktyupakramaṁ dyotayati।).

140 “anyathā” iti । vimarśaśaktyā vinā mayūrāṇḍarasasya yā
nīlapītādinirbharatā sā yathā kāraṇatā, na tu kartr̥tā tathā bhagavato
’pi aśeṣaviśvanirbharatā syāt, na ca kāraṇatāmātraṁ yuktam iti bhāvaḥ ।
— ĪPVV II: 205.

141 As I pointed out in the n 101 above, Utpala’s treatment is quite in
line with his singular speaking definition of agency in terms of action
whose essence is “savouring” (camatkāra) characterized by supreme
luminosity and joy: īśvarasya anantakartr̥tā camatkārarūpā kriyoktā
paramaprakāśānandamayī । (Vr̥tti on 4.1.6). I am grateful to Torella for
bringing this portion of the Vr̥tti to light. I however take
camatkārarūpā as qualifying paramaprakāśānandamayī kriyā. Even
otherwise, when camatkārarūpā is taken as an adjective of anantakartr̥tā,
it has to be predicated by kriyā. It may be remembered that the Vivr̥ti
does not take cognizance of this formulation in the Vr̥tti and also that
the context here lacks any aesthetic reference. It is however immaterial
since the primary conceptual focus of Utpala remains consistently the
same.

142 “pūrṇa” iti [. . .] yaś camatkāra ānandātmā paramo bhogaḥ । — ĪPVV
I: 85-86; see n 81 above.

143 “camatkr̥tirūpatvāt” iti ānandātmakaparamabhogaparyantā viśrāntir eva
hi viśuddhaṁ svātantryaṁ yatra pratighātaleśakr̥tam asvātantryaṁ manāg
api na śakyaṁ yena ’ātmānam ata eva’ iti (1.5.15), “svātantryāmuktam”
(1.5.16) ityādi vicitraṁ nirmāṇaṁ vakṣyamāṇam abhivyanakti । tataḥ [. .
.] hr̥dayaṁ sā bhagavataḥ । [. . .] “hr̥dayam eva” iti na atra upacaritaṁ
hr̥dayatvam, api tu mukhyam eveti bhāvaḥ । — ĪPVV II: 205-06

144 The Vr̥tti is glossing aham idam ity akhaṇḍāmarśabr̥ṁhitaḥ from the
Kārikā with akhaṇḍitagrāhakākhaṇḍitagrāhyamelanāca-matkāropabr̥ṁhitaḥ. I
will refer the reader for text and translation to Torella (1994: 73,
210) and also to the copious citations from the Vivr̥ti glossing the
Vr̥tti text. In his excellent translation Torella connects camatkāra with
each of the three components of the lengthy phrase (mahāpadaṁ as
Abhinavagupta calls it) namely akaṇḍitagrāhaka, akhaṇḍitagrāhya and
tanmelanā (“savouring” of the undivided perceiving subject, of the
undivided perceptible object and of the fusion of the two). I am however
inclined to think slightly differently. I take akaṇḍitagrāhaka as the
Vr̥tti’s explanation of ahaṁ, akhaṇḍitagrāhya as that of idam and
tanmelanācamatkāraḥ as that of akhaṇḍāmarśa. Unfortunately there is no
citation from the Vivr̥ti in ĪPVV on this kārikā on camatkāra, nor does
Abhinavagupta offer any comment, hence this interpretation may be of
only tentative value.

145 Initially I had my reservations going over and over again over
Abhinavagupta’s thorough insights, I am now convinced that he sees
Somānanda as Utpala’s inspiration behind initiating the latter’s notion
of camatkāra and also that Abhinavagupta’s comprehensive build up of
camatkāra as quintessence of his own aesthetic theory is fully
sanctioned and endorsed by his core scholastic tradition. See also n
109.

146 Torella translates it as “conscious beautitude”.

147 (tat yuktam uktaṁ) “tathā svayaṁ prakāśate” iti । — ĪPVV II: 181

148 evaṁ pāramārthikam aham iti prakāśasya nirvr̥tirūpaṁ vapuḥ । — ĪPVV
II: 181. In my rendering pāramārthikaṁ qualifies vapuḥ. Alternatively it
could also qualify ahaṁ.

149 Vide ibid.: 180-81.

150 Vide “camatkr̥ter abhāvāt” [. . .] “viśeṣaprītiḥ” [. . .] “anyathā”:
extracts from Vivr̥ti. — ĪPVV II: 179.

151 “anyathā” iti (yadi pramātari viśrāmyed ity arthaḥ । etac ca ‘kintu
mohavaśāt’ — 1.1.3 iti sūtre prakāśavimarśavailakṣaṇyaṁ nirūpitam eva)
“vimarśaḥ punar na kadācid idantābhūmim āskandati” (ity atra avasare) ।
“tathā ca” iti (yataḥ sarvo vimarśaḥ pramātary eva viśrāntim āśrayate
tena prakāreṇa ।) — ĪPVV II: 179-80. It should be noted that (i)
Abhinavagupta is demonstrating Utpala’s deliberate attempt to revisit
the contents of 1.1.3 and see 1.5.11 as continuation thereof; (ii) the
text, e.g. vimarśaḥ punar na kadācid idantābhūmim āskandati, appearing
as part of the ĪPVV, is actually from the Vivr̥ti and appears on p. 83,
vol. I of the ĪPVV; (iii) tathā ca from the Vivr̥ti recapitulates what
has been said in the Vivr̥ti on 1.1.3; and (iv) this tathā ca is
recaptured and re-endorsed by tathā in “tathā svayaṁ prakāśate” (II:
181) which is the starting point of the present discussion.

152 (tat katham uktaṁ) “vimarśo na kadācid idantābhūmim āskandati” (ity
āśaṅkyāha) “svato ’pi viśuddhasya” iti । [. . .] “śuddhasya” iti
(kartari ṣaṣṭhī) । śuddhatvena [. . .] (pramātur viśeṣa ukto ’ham ity
avicchedaprakāśaviśrāntilakṣaṇaḥ । ata eva camato bhuñjānasya karaṇaṁ
saṁrambho ’ham asau nīlāder bhokteti ।) [. . .] (atra hetuḥ) “jaḍa” iti
। (jaḍāt hi cetanasya vimarśakr̥ta eva viśeṣaḥ । — ĪPVV I: 83. Here, as
pointed out in the preceding note, the sentence vimarśaḥ [. . .]

154 tac ca yatra āropyate, tad api nirvr̥timayam eva bhāti śarīraprāṇādi
। tad āha “aham ity api” iti । — ĪPVV II: 181

155 sā cāvighnā saṁvit camatkāras tajjo ’pi
kampapulakollaka-sanādirvikāraḥ camatkāraḥ । — ABh I: 279; also see n 84
above.

156 tathāhi duḥkham api ayam āsvādayamāno ’ntar eva viśrāmyati । tāṁ tu
antarviśrāntiṁ bāhyena arthī taṁ ca vinaṣṭam abhisamīkṣamāṇo
’nādaratiraskr̥tatvāt satīm api na abhimanute । upadeśaviśeṣadiśā tu
māyāvigalane tadbāhyarūpānādaraṇe saiva viśrāntir unmiṣati । tathā hi
śivadr̥ṣṭiḥ “duḥkhe’pi pravikāsena” ityādi । — ŚD 5.9c. The citation by
Abhinavagupta calls for a few comments. By suffixing ityādi (instead of
usual iti he implies inclusion of the immediately following related
discussion in the ŚD. The relative two verses run like this: duḥkhādinā
viśeṣaś cet tatrāpy aśivatā na ca । duḥkhe ’pi pravikāsena duḥkhārthe
dhr̥tisaṁgamāt ।। dr̥śyate bandhanaṁ teṣu dhr̥tiḥ kīṭādike tathā । nirvr̥tyā
yatra yogo ’sti tatraiva śivatā sphuṭam ।। — ŚD 5.9-10. [(Should someone
doubt that) pain will entail difference/change in the blissful nature of
Śiva. (The answer is) No, it will not lead to loss of śivatā (i.e.
essential nature of Śiva). Since in pain also endurance (dhr̥ti)
constantly marks the object/source of pain through the blossoming (of
the essential nature of Śiva). (I do not translate the third line).
Wherever there is contact with sublime blissfulness (nirvr̥ti) śivatā is
indeed visible right there.] The sole purpose behind this long quotation
is to underline the essential connection between nirvr̥ti and pravikāsa
and the rationale behind Abhinavagupta’s deliberate choice of this
passage from the ŚD while alluding to nirvr̥ti as a precursor idea of
Utpala’s camatkāra.

In fact, Somānanda in the current context is laying down the doctrine of
consciousness-expansion or, in other words, that of the expanded
consciousness which is going to prove central to Utpala’s and
Abhinavagupta’s aesthetic insights. Just a few, precisely two, verses
prior in the ŚD he delineates that all the instances and categories of
phenomenal existence, which even when constrained by contraction in
themselves, remain always endowed with expansiveness, joyfulness and
blissfulness (sarve svātmaparicchedavanto nityam avasthitāḥ ।
vikāsāhlādavantaś ca sarve nirvr̥tiyoginaḥ) ।। — ŚD 5.6 (See Torella
1994: 90, n 3, for alluding to this kārikā in a different context.) This
hemistich (duḥkhe ’pi pravikāsena) actually proved to be a catalyst of
whole chain of thought from Somānanda to Abhinavagupta in viewing
categories of all descriptions not only in the objective world but also
in the subjective realm as modes of expanded consciousness flooded with
joy. In the Parātriṁśikā-Vivaraṇa Abhinavagupta holds in unequivocal
terms that the states of anger, delusion, etc. happen to be an
expression of the absolutic consciousness and leans back on Somānanda
for support citing him thrice, the final quotation being: duḥkhe ’pi
pravikāsena sthairyārthe dhr̥tisaṁgamāt । — (ŚD 5.9cd) [In pain also the
purpose is steadiness through the efflorescence of the essential nature
by means of endurance” — tr. Jaideva Singh.] Abhinavagupta concludes
that the states of anger, etc. exist because of their being one with
savouring of/by consciousness (ciccamatkāra), otherwise they would not
be able even to attain their nature: [. . .] krodhamohādivr̥ttayo hi
paripūrṇabhagavadbhairavabhaṭṭārakasaṁvidātmikā eva । yad uktaṁ
śrīsomānandapādaiḥ [. . .] ’duḥkhe ’pi pravikāsena sthairyārthe
(duḥkhārthe KSS) dhr̥tisaṁgamāt ।’ ityādi । — PTV: 41-42. Moving forward,
Abhinavagupta continues to enlarge upon the concept lending to it more
depth on the aesthetic side. According to him, complete lack of
savouring, i.e. delight, means insentiency. Absorption in the profuse
delightful savouring alone marks the aesthetic susceptibility
(sahr̥dayatā). In grief also, there is the same savouring experience.
Grief, having fully thickened (vikāsam āpannaṁ: matured, expanded) due
to utter despair, turns into delightful experience (because of the
expansion of its essential nature as consciousness). At this point,
Abhinavagupta looks up to the same statement of Somānanda: duḥkhe’pi
pravikāsena (ŚD 5.9c): sarvato hi acamatkāre jaḍataiva
adhikacamatkārāveśa eva [. . .] sahr̥dayatā ucyate [. . .] duḥkhe ’pi eṣa
eva camatkāraḥ, [. . .] tad eva [. . .] vikāsam āpannaṁ iti
nairapekṣyavaśasaviśeṣacamatkriyātmaduḥkhasatattvam । taduktam ’duḥkhe
’pi pravikāsena’ iti । — PTV: 49.

Abhinavagupta’s obsession with this formulaton of Somānanda is so
deep-rooted that he leaves no opportunity towards dwelling on this idea
sourcing the same to precisely this kārikā. What makes it all the more
interesting is that he does so in the contexts provided by Utpala. Under
ĪPK 1.4.5 Abhinavagupta is discussing the Vivr̥ti’s relegation of the
notion of pleasure, propounded by the Pramāṇa-Viniścaya, as belonging to
the subject identified with intellect (buddhipramātā) who is not a real
subject (vāstavapramātā): (yad āha) “idam eva hi naḥ sukhaṁ yat sātaṁ
saṁvedanam” iti । (etat pratikṣipati) “na ca” iti । [. . .] “vāstava”
iti [. . .] (tata uktaṁ) “na sukhitvādi” iti । — ĪPVV II: 47-49. On the
contrary the real subject even during the worldly existence (where the
empirical subject gets occasional entrenchment in his intrinsic
spiritual identity) has a feel of inner joy even when he undergoes
painful experience. In fact, pain and pleasure become synonymous in such
a situation. Explaining the word tadā (then) Abhinavagupta quotes ŚD 5.9
and also highlights that this happens because of the specialized
training or teaching (upadeśabalena). In so doing he brings the insights
of the VBh and ŚD on the same axis: “tadā” iti
(vāstavapramātrāveśadaśāyāṁ, yadi vā) “tadā” iti (sukhaduḥkhotpādāvasare
yo lokasya tatra tatra । te hi “saṁbandhe sāvadhānatā ।’ [VBh 106] ity
upadeśabalena svarūpavimarśadraḍhimani viśrāmyantaḥ parānandena yujyante
। yathoktam ‘ānande mahati prāpte dr̥ṣṭe vā bāndhave cirāt ।’ [VBh 71]
ityādi ‘duḥkhe ’pi ca vikāse ’pi sthairyārthaṁ dhr̥tisaṁgamāt ।’ [ŚD 5.9]
ityādi ca) । — ĪPVV II: 50

157 tata eva hr̥dayena parāmarśalakṣaṇena prādhānyāt vyapadeśyā
vyavasthitasya api prakāśabhāgasya vedyaviśrāntasya anādaraṇāt
sahr̥dayatā ucyate । — ibid.: 178-79

158 pramātr̥bhāgam eva pradhānatayā vimr̥śan bhuñjāna iti ucyate । —
ibid.: 178.

159 From now on Abhinavagupta does not name Somānanda. My only reason in
believing that he is presenting his great grand-teacher’s view is
because of its contextual placement. It begins just after citation from
Somānanda as a part of the continuing narrative of Utpala that begins
with “tathā svayaṁ prakāśate” iti । [. . .] (tad āha) “aham ity api” iti
। and closes just after presentation of these views: (etad upasaṁharati)
“tadevam” iti । This is followed by (etat saṁvādayati) “tathā ca” iti
“bhartr̥harir ity avocat” iti (saṁbandhaḥ). Abhinavagupta wants us to
appreciate that Utpala is demonstrating full backing of the tradition
that he enjoys. The ideas of Somānanda figure as endorsement from his
own tradition and those of Bhartr̥hari as that from a different but
complementing ally.

160 ye tu śarīrādititīkṣavas te taditarasvargādigataśarīravāñchayā vā
taccharīrāvinābhāviniṣpratīkāraduḥkhajihāsayā aham iti camatkārādhyāsaṁ
tatra śarīre śithilayanti, śarīrāntare ca taṁ camatkārāgraham

161 ity ayam artho ’bhinavabhāratyāṁ nāṭyavedavivr̥tau vitatya vyutpādito

162 [. . .] nirvighnāḥ svādarūpāś ca rasanāḥ tadgocarīkāryāś
cittavr̥ttayo rasā nava ity ayam artho ’bhinavabhāratyāṁ [. . .] vitatya
vyutpāditaḥ [. . .] । — ibid. The printed text is rasanātad° which I
separate as above. I am guided by Pandey’s MS reading cited in Pandey
1950: 94(n1), 422: [. . .] nirvighnāsvādarūpā ca rasanā ।
tadgocarīkāryāḥ [. . .]. । In the second edition he retains this reading
though he refers to the KSTS edition of ĪPVV [Pandey 1959: 141, 639]
without highlighting this deviation. Left to me, I prefer Pandey’s
reading which has rasanā as a generic term. Here rasanāḥ in plural takes
an analytic view of the same with reference to their respective content.
The plural in rasanāḥ is necessitated by plural in nirvighnāḥ and
svādarūpāśca which qualify and describe it.

what is pictured up in imagination and pleasure etc., shine externally,

172 ullekhaś ca utprekṣaṇena svavyāpāreṇa utkīryeva arthād utkr̥ṣyeva
likhyamānaś citrakalpaś chāyāmātrarūpo vā । — ĪPVV I: 252

173 vikalpe yo ’yam ullekhaḥ so ’pi bāhyaḥ pr̥thakprathaḥ ।
pra-mātraikātmyamāntaryaṁ tato bhedo hi bāhyatā ।। — ĪPK 1.8.8; vikalpe
ghaṭādyullekhaś cakṣurādyagocaro ’pi pr̥thagābhāsād bāhya eva ।
ahaṁvimarśo hy āntaratvam, idam iti tu bāhyatā । evaṁ ca ghaṭānām ubhayī
bāhyatā bāhyāntaḥkaraṇadvayīvedyatā, sukhādes tv ekāntaḥkaraṇavedyataiva
। — ĪPVr̥ thereon. [Even the mental representation (ullekha) which occurs
in the discursive thought (vikalpe) is external, because it appears in
terms of separation (pr̥thakprathaḥ). Indeed, internality is unity with
the knowing subject (pramātraikyam), externality is separation.

The mental representation of the jar, etc. in the sphere of discursive
thought, though not the object of the senses such as light, is none the
less external so far as it is manifested as separate (from the subject).
Indeed, internality is the reflective awareness “I” (ahaṁvimarśaḥ),
externality is the reflective awareness “this”. Thus for things — such
as the jar and so on — there are two kinds of externality, i.e. they are
the objects of cognition through both the external and internal senses;
for pleasure and so on there is one, in that they are cognized only by
the internal sense. — tr. Torella.]

174 “iti” iti yat pūrvam uktaṁ tato hetor idam avasthitaṁ yad
anubhūyamānasya smaryamāṇasya samullikhyamānasya ca ghaṭābhāsasya
vāstavam ekatvaṁ yogyatayā ca dvitayī bāhyatā asty eva bāhyatayā
bāhyendriyavedyatayā ceti । — ĪPVV II: 429. Mark the parity between
anubhūyamāna and samullikhyamāna.

175 nanu bāhyatādvitvanivedane sūtroktabāhyatāvibhāgaprakhyāpanayā
avaśyavaktavye “ghaṭādīnām” iti viśeṣaṇasya kiṁ vyāvartyam ity āśaṅkya
yā vr̥ttiḥ “sukhādes tu” ityādikā, tāṁ vivr̥ṇoti “sukhādeḥ punaḥ” iti ।
“aviśeṣa”-padapr̥ṣṭhe “mātra”-grahaṇaṁ dhyānollikhitagaruḍādau
pramātrantarāsādhāraṇyāviśeṣe ’pi yad ullikhitam aparaṁ bāhyatvam asti,
tad api nāsti sukhādau iti prakaṭīkartum । — ĪPVV II: 429. [The editors
of the KSS edition do not print ghaṭādīnām, aviśeṣa and mātra in bold
and within inverted commas as is their wont in indicating the Vivr̥ti’s
or Vr̥tti’s text. It is difficult to figure out the exact phrase in the
Vivr̥ti as regards aviśeṣa and mātra, but there is no doubt that they are
from the Vivr̥ti.] The long quotation makes it very clear that
Abhinavagupta is very emphatic in pointing out to this subtle
distinction within the two orders of mental entities and that this
distinction was made by Utpala himself. Despite the fact that the image
that appears in meditation is not open to other percipients like other
mental episodes, it is qualitatively different from pleasure, etc. as
the latter lacks the characterstic of (even potential)
sense-perceptibility which the former is endowed with.

176 ullekhasukhaduḥkhalajjādīnāṁ cākṣuṣān(emend ṇ)āṁ rūpādīnām iva
īśvarasya śaktyā sākṣātkaraṇarūpo bauddhaḥ prakāśaḥ । — ĪPVr̥ on 1.8.9
[The manifestation (prakāśaḥ) of mental representations and pleasure,
pain, shame, etc. that takes place through the mind (bauddaḥ) is no
different from that which, having the form of direct perception due to
the power of the Lord concerns those realities that can be perceived by
the eye, such as forms/colours, etc. — tr. Torella with modification.]

177 Vide, nn 163 and 176 above.

178 dr̥ṣṭāntaṁ vyācaṣṭe “cakṣurādi” iti । — ĪPVV II: 430

179 gāḍham ullikhyamāne tu vikalpena sukhādike । tathāsthitis tathaiva
syāt sphuṭam asyopalakṣaṇāt ।। — ĪPK1.8.4 [However, when the
(manifestation) pleasure, etc. is reproduced owing to intensive
repetition through mental discursion (vikalpena) then that state (of the
subject) comes about (in the subject) because of the distinctively vivid
experience of that (asya, i.e. pleasure manifestation: sukhābhāsa) — tr.
Torella, with modifications. (I take the genitive asya as karma, not
kartā, of upalakṣaṇa).] Vr̥tti on the above: kutaścit prayatnaviśeṣāt
svatantravikalpollikhitaṁ sphuṭam eva sukhādijātaṁ vikāsādihetuḥ । —
[Reproduced through an autonomous mental discursion with certain
(kutaścid) particular (type of) effort (prayatnaviśeṣād) pleasure etc.
becomes completely vivid and causes joy (lit. expansion), etc. — tr.
Torella, with modifications.]

180 According to the Vivr̥ti, ullekhana is re-vivification, i.e.
re-living in the present: “pratyupasthāpyate” iti vartamānābhāsīkriyate
। — ĪPVV, II: 410

181 na hi tadā asau smaryamāṇaḥ, api tu vartamāna eva sukhādyābhāsaḥ । —
ibid.

182 sukhābhāsalakṣaṇaṁ yat sāmānyasmaraṇena upasthāpitaṁ, tad eva yataḥ
sphuṭīkaroti nirantaro vikalpaḥ । — ĪPVV, II: 411. Without going into
detailed discussion on the perception-like characteristics of memory due
to repeated attention and its identification with meditation
(dhyānādibhāvaṁ smr̥tir eva labdhvā cintāmaṇis tadvibhavaṁ vyanakti —
cited in ĪPV I: 154 on ĪPK 1.4.1) and the capacity of autonomous mental
construct to evoke a fresh new perceptual experience of its object again
owing to pointed unbroken concentration, it seems to me that the fence
between the two modes of cognition and their respective objects is very
hazy. Utpala revisits the problem in his Vr̥tti and Vivr̥ti both on 1.4.2
and Abhinavagupta in his ĪPVV thereon. Abhinavagupta addresses this
issue once again in his ĪPV where he refers to the element of “absence
of openness to other percipients” (anyasādhāraṇyānavabhāsāt — ĪPV I:
160) being the catalyst of distinction between extremely vivid memory
and autonomous mental construct in visualizing the deity. But as
Abhinavagupta himself would have it that “absence of openness to other
percipients” is equally common to the autonomous mental construct and
meditational activity (dhyānollikhitagaruḍādau
pramātrantarāsādhāraṇyāviśeṣe ’pi — ĪPVV II: 429) as well, this
criterion becomes suspect and calls for further scrutiny. What is
extremely intriguing is that Utpala remains non-committal about his
stand whether this perception-like modality he considers logically
determinate or indeterminate (tadā ca vikalpatā syāt nirvikalpatā veti
nāsmākaṁ grahaḥ — Vivr̥ti on 1.4.2, in Torella 2007b: 548). The reason
for my taking a special call in this context comes from Abhinavagupta
himself, when in the ABh on NŚ 6.31 during the course of mooting the
notion of camatkāra he notices some kind of homology between mental
ascertainment in the nature of perception, retrospecting (saṅkalpa) and
memory with a pointed reference to Kālidāsa’s usage of the word smarati
(“remembers”): sa ca sākṣātkārasvabhāvo mānaso ’dhyavasāyo vā saṅkalpo
vā smr̥tir vā tathātvena sphurann astu । yad āha “ramyāṇi vīkṣya” [. . .]
tac cetasā smarati [. . .] (Śākuntala 5.2) (see Gnoli 1968: 60-61, for
translation and explanatory notes). Also see Rastogi 2012: 400-01.

183 Utpala wants his reader to know that this vividness that depends
upon the sense-contact or sense-perception contemporaneous with the
object (tātkālikākṣasāmakṣyasāpekṣāḥ — ĪPK 1.8.1) can be brought about
by intensely continuous concentration or attention alone by taking
resort to certain particularly dedicated effort for this purpose.
Utpala’s deployment of the phrase kutaścid prayatnaviśeṣāt in the Vr̥tti
(perhaps explaining tathaiva in the Kārikā) and the way it is exegeted
by the Vivr̥ti leaves no doubt about his intention: “kutaścit” iti
(vyadhikaraṇatayā pañcamyarthaṁ vyācaṣṭe) “upadeśa” ityādinā “āhitam”
ityantena । (kandasauṣumṇapadādyanupraveśaḥ upadeśaviśeṣaḥ,
āsaṅgo’nurāgaḥ, anyo heturadr̥ṣṭādiḥ) — ĪPVV II: 410. Here the Vivr̥ti
refers to its happening due to definitive teachings of certain yogic
practices or due to deep attachment/love or due to some other factors
such as unseen destiny (adr̥ṣṭa). The word “etc.” (ādi) highlights the
open-endedness of the usage. There is a motive to such an approach. The
prayatnaviśeṣa, we will see, covers the pheomena of intense devotion,
meditation and visualization through art. What is necessary is that
whatever the effort, the projection must be vivid as if occurring in the
present before the eyes and that it must be noticed/grasped
distinctively. The word upalakṣaṇāt is a pointer to it. If we recall
upalakṣite in ĪPK 1.1.3, the heart-filling nature of this perceptual
experience will come to the fore.

184 I agree with Torella’s rendering of vikāsa as joy (in virtue of its
being the arthakriyā of sukha). Gnoli (1968: 46-47) translates it with
“expansion” while rendering Abhinavagupta’s portion of the ABh on Bhaṭṭa
Nāyaka. The three states of mind (citta), namely, fluidity, enlargement
and expansion (druti, vistāra and vikāsa respectively:
drūtivistāravikāsalakṣaṇena [. . .] bhogena । — ABh I: 177) form
important part of any literary theory on rasa-perception. What Utpala
wanted to imply by the word ādi (etc.) in vikāsādihetuḥ is anybody’s
guess today. Did he include druti and vistāra also? Nobody knows. The
ĪPVV does not offer any clue. However, two conclusions could be drawn
safely: (i) vikāsa here does pertain to joy or joyous state of mind
owing to the immediate centrality of sukha (pleasure, joy) in the
present discussion: sukhādes tu yāvatī arthakriyā vivakṣitā sā pūrṇaiva
। (ĪPVV II: 411); and (ii) Somānanda and Utpala are fully familiar with
this notion of vikāsa. Former’s preoccupation with this notion has been
discussed in connection with camatkāra with special reference to ŚD 5.9.
However, he directly relates vikāsa (or vikāsitā) with power of joy or
blissful sublimity. Utpala makes it abundantly clear that proclivity
towards unqualified joy is vikāsa or state of expansiveness:
tatkarmanirvr̥tiprāptir aunmukhyaṁ tadvikāsitā । — ŚD 1.20ab; Utpala
comments: karmāvacchinnā nirvr̥ttir aunmukhyam anavacchinnanirvr̥timātram
ānandaśaktir iti yāvat । tad aunmukhyaṁ vikāsitā cidāsthāpravikāsa ity
uktam — ŚDVr̥: 17. Utpala clearly has ŚD 1.15 in his mind: bodhasya
svātmaniṣṭhasya racanāṁ prati nirvr̥tiḥ । tadāsthāpravikāso yas tad
aunmukhyaṁ pracakṣate ।।

185 Vide p. 100 and n 3 above.

186 Kumari S.S. Janaki agrees with me. Janaki in fact clinches the
issue. She emphatically concludes that the only compelling reason behind
Ruyyaka’s recognition of ullekha as a distinct figure was Utpala’s
phenomenological thought as propounded in his ĪPK. For Ruyyaka actually
encountered “trouble to establish it as a separate figure distinct from
rūpaka, bhrāntimān, atiśayokti and śleṣa with all of which it is found
in combination. Ruyyaka’s difficulty in this aspect is quite evident
from his discussion of the figure of speech in the passage yas tapovanam
iti munibhiḥ etc. from Harṣacarita quoted by him in this context.
According to Ruyyaka, the peculiar feature of viewing the same object in
different ways is to be seen only in ullekha. Even if it is found in
combination with any other figure, there is only the possibility of a
saṁkara of different alaṁkāras. Even in that case the need to recognize
ullekha as a distinct figure is not obviated. [. . .] Taking this cue
from Ruyyaka, later writers like Vidyādhara (Ekāvalī, p. 227), Appayya
Dīkṣita (Citra-Mīmāṁsā, p. 67) and Jagannātha Paṇḍita (Rasa-Gaṅgādhara,
p. 358) accept two varieties of ullekha — śuddha and saṅkīr̥ṇa. The
question naturally arises as to how and why the same object could be or
is viewed in different ways. In this connection Ruyyaka quotes the
verse: “yathāruci [. . .] °sādhite” which is [. . .] a śloka from [. .
.] Utpaladeva.” — Janaki 1965: 97-98

Alaṁkāra-Sarvasva, sūtra 19, p. 58 (NŚ edition).

224 pākarūpayā samyagyojanayā (tāvad alaukiko raso jāyate) । — ABh I:
288; pākakrameṇa samyagyojanārūpāt kuśalasaṁpādyāt saṁyogāt — ibid.;
also see Pandey 1935: 22

225 vibhāvādināmadheyavyapadeśyaiḥ [. . .] sāmājikadhiyi samyagyogaṁ
saṁbandham aikāgryaṁ vā āsāditavadbhir alau-
kikanirvighnasaṁvedanātmakacarvaṇāgocaratāṁ nīto ’rthaḥ [. . .]
sthāyivilakṣaṇa eva rasaḥ ABh I: 284. Abhinavagupta’s above-noted
explanation of saṁyoga is a bit intriguing. Gnoli takes all the three,
saṁyagyoga (perfect combination), saṁbandha (relation) and aikāgrya
(conspiration) together (Gnoli 1968: 78). I however beg to differ from
him, the reason being that with the use of vā, Abhinavagupta makes all
the senses optional or alternable. I have one more difference. I take
saṁyagyoga as a semantic analysis of saṁyoga — the adjective samyak
qualifying yoga, whereas saṁbandha (relation) and aikāgrya
(one-pointedness) as the alternative or optional meaning/explanation of
yoga.

234 I will refer the learned reader to the landmark article (pp. 1-2,
20-23) of Gopinath Kaviraj way back in 1923-24 titled “The Doctrine of
Pratibhā in Indian Philosophy” (ABORI, Poona) reprinted in the Aspects
of Indian Thought (pp. 1-43), 1966. Kaviraj specially notes the level of
profoundity shown by the Trika system.

235 “Thus in the Pratyabhijñāhr̥daya (p. 52) Kṣemarajā quotes a verse1
[1yā caiṣā pratibhā tattatpadārthakramarūṣitā । akramānantacidrūpaḥ
pramātā sa maheśvaraḥ ।।] in which pratibhā is identified with the
supreme subject, whose nature is infinite intelligence (anantacidrūpa),
indivisible and void of time-limitation (akrama). It is unalterable and
everywhere One, being the one Light whose reflection shines through
every state of modal consciousness.” — ibid.: 22-23. The kārikā cited by
Kaviraj is ĪPK 1.7.1

236 sarasvatī svādu tadarthavastu niḥṣyandamānā mahatāṁ kavīnām ।
alokasāmānyam abhivyanakti pratisphurantaṁ pratibhāviśeṣam ।। — DhĀ 1.6
[Sarasvatī, (working) within great poets, in pouring forth this sweet
matter (arthavastu) (viz. the emotions and flavors) reveals a special,
vibrant, genius (pratibhā), which is superhuman. — tr. Ingalls, etc.]

237 sarasvatīti vāgrūpā bhagavatītyarthaḥ । [. . .] abhivyanakti
pratisphurantam iti (parisphurantam variant), pratipattṝn prati sā
pratibhā nānumīyamānā, api tu tadāveśena bhāsamānā ity arthaḥ । yad
uktam asmadupādhyāyabhaṭṭatautena – ‘nāyakasya kaveḥ śrotuḥ samāno
’nubhavas tataḥ’ iti । pratibhā apūrvavastunirmāṇakṣamā prajñā । tasyā
viśeṣo rasāveśavaiśadyasaundaryakāvyanirmāṇakṣamatvam । [. . .] yeneti ।
abhivyaktena sphuratā pratibhāviśeṣeṇa nimittena mahākavitvagaṇaneti
yāvat । — DhĀL: 23-24

238 prajñā navanavonmeṣaśālinī pratibhā matā । tadanuprāṇanā
jīvadvarṇanānipuṇaḥ kaviḥ ।।

248 tata eva bhaṭṭacandrānandena “paśyato rūpam ālekhād bhāto

abhidhāya api “ātmasaṁśrayā” iti saṁvidviśrāntataiva uktā । — ibid.

249 na kevalaṁ ca saṁbandhamayaikatāsparśiparimitamāyāpramā-
tr̥paryavasāyī vastuprakāśanavyāpāro yāvad
anantacinmaya-śivatābhāsaviśrāntaiva pratikṣaṇaṁ vastusaṁvitkriyā । na
ca saṁvidaḥ kramo ’sti ekaiva ekatraiva kṣaṇe arthaprakāśatā
saṁbandhaśivatāmayam eva svarūpam āviṣkaroti ity ucyate । tathā
cācāryadharmakīrtiḥ “bahirmukhaṁ ca tajjñānaṁ bhāty arthapratibhāsavat ।
buddheś ca grāhikā buddhir nityam antarmukhātmani ।” (Pramāṇavārttika
2.428) iti antarmukhā iti, ātmani iti ca svasaṁvedanam arthābhāsaśūnyam
apr̥thagbhūtaṁ coktam । tathā pratyabhijñāyām uktam “yā caiṣā pratibhā
tattatpadārthakramarūṣitā । akramānantacidrūpaḥ pramātā sa maheśvaraḥ
।।” (ĪPK 1.7.1) ity etat tatraiva vitatya pradarśitam । —
Saṁbandha-Siddhi, p. 9.

From n 248 and the citation from Pramāṇavārttika as above it is evident
that Utpala’s formulation of pratibhā was impacted by Bhaṭṭa Candrānanda
and Dharmakīrti, but this is beyond our immediate purview.

250 tattatpadārthakramarūṣitā.

251 [. . .] atyarthabhinnārthāvabhāsakhacite vibhau.

252 yā caiṣā pratibhā tattatpadārthakramarūṣitā । akramānantacidrūpaḥ
pramātā sa maheśvaraḥ ।। [And that (which is this) inner manifestive
awareness (pratibhā) inlaid with (rūṣitā) succession of myriad objects
is the knowing subject — free from succession, delimitation and having
consciousness as (his) nature — Maheśvara.] This kārikā is open to
different renderings depending upon what one follows, e.g. Kārikā, Vr̥tti
or Vivr̥ti. For now, it is following Kārikā as I have been able to
understand it.

253 yā eṣā pratibhā sa pramātā maheśvaraḥ ।

254 eṣā pratibhā pramātā maheśvaraś ca ।

255 yā pratibhā eṣā sa maheśvaraḥ pramātā ।

256 (yā) pramātā maheśvaraḥ sā pratibhā ।

257 antarlīnārthābhāsa abstracted from “antarlīnatadarthābhāsasya
atad-vyapohanena” (Vr̥tti on 1.6.3) and supported by Abhinavagupta’s
antaḥsarvārthāvabhāsaḥ (ĪPV on 1.6.3).

258 Vide pramātur eva svatantrasya antarlīnatadatadarthābhāsasya
atadapohanena [. . .] । — ĪPVr̥ 1.6.3.

259 “yā pratibhā eṣā” iti sarvasya sadaiva svaprakāśā vastutaś ca
